At the same time, the agents drive in a human-like manner, as measured by their similarity to existing human driving logs. We also find that HR-PPO agents show considerable improvements on proxy measures for coordination with human driving, particularly in highly interactive scenarios. We open-source our code and trained agents at https://github.com/Emerge-Lab/nocturne_lab and provide demonstrations of agent behaviors at https://sites.google.com/view/driving-partners.</p></details> |  |
| **[Motion planning for off-road autonomous driving based on human-like cognition and weight adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.17820v1)** | 2024-04-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Driving in an off-road environment is challenging for autonomous vehicles due to the complex and varied terrain. To ensure stable and efficient travel, the vehicle requires consideration and balancing of environmental factors, such as undulations, roughness, and obstacles, to generate optimal trajectories that can adapt to changing scenarios. However, traditional motion planners often utilize a fixed cost function for trajectory optimization, making it difficult to adapt to different driving strategies in challenging irregular terrains and uncommon scenarios. To address these issues, we propose an adaptive motion planner based on human-like cognition and cost evaluation for off-road driving. First, we construct a multi-layer map describing different features of off-road terrains, including terrain elevation, roughness, obstacle, and artificial potential field map. Subsequently, we employ a CNN-LSTM network to learn the trajectories planned by human drivers in various off-road scenarios. Then, based on human-like generated trajectories in different environments, we design a primitive-based trajectory planner that aims to mimic human trajectories and cost weight selection, generating trajectories that are consistent with the dynamics of off-road vehicles. Finally, we compute optimal cost weights and select and extend behavioral primitives to generate highly adaptive, stable, and efficient trajectories. We validate the effectiveness of the proposed method through experiments in a desert off-road environment with complex terrain and varying road conditions. The experimental results show that the proposed human-like motion planner has excellent adaptability to different off-road conditions. It shows real-time operation, greater stability, and more human-like planning ability in diverse and challenging scenarios.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond Imitation: A Life-long Policy Learning Framework for Path Tracking Control of Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.17198v1)** | 2024-04-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Model-free learning-based control methods have recently shown significant advantages over traditional control methods in avoiding complex vehicle characteristic estimation and parameter tuning. As a primary policy learning method, imitation learning (IL) is capable of learning control policies directly from expert demonstrations. However, the performance of IL policies is highly dependent on the data sufficiency and quality of the demonstrations. To alleviate the above problems of IL-based policies, a lifelong policy learning (LLPL) framework is proposed in this paper, which extends the IL scheme with lifelong learning (LLL). First, a novel IL-based model-free control policy learning method for path tracking is introduced. Even with imperfect demonstration, the optimal control policy can be learned directly from historical driving data. Second, by using the LLL method, the pre-trained IL policy can be safely updated and fine-tuned with incremental execution knowledge. Third, a knowledge evaluation method for policy learning is introduced to avoid learning redundant or inferior knowledge, thus ensuring the performance improvement of online policy learning. Experiments are conducted using a high-fidelity vehicle dynamic model in various scenarios to evaluate the performance of the proposed method. The results show that the proposed LLPL framework can continuously improve the policy performance with collected incremental driving data, and achieves the best accuracy and control smoothness compared to other baseline methods after evolving on a 7 km curved road. Through learning and evaluation with noisy real-life data collected in an off-road environment, the proposed LLPL framework also demonstrates its applicability in learning and evolving in real-life scenarios.</p></details> |  |
| **[Stay on Track: A Frenet Wrapper to Overcome Off-road Trajectories in Vehicle Motion Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.00605v2)** | 2024-04-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Predicting the future motion of observed vehicles is a crucial enabler for safe autonomous driving. The field of motion prediction has seen large progress recently with state-of-the-Art (sotA) models achieving impressive results on large-scale public benchmarks. However, recent work revealed that learning-based methods are prone to predict off-road trajectories in challenging scenarios. These can be created by perturbing existing scenarios with additional turns in front of the target vehicle while the motion history is left unchanged. We argue that this indicates that SotA models do not consider the map information sufficiently and demonstrate how this can be solved by representing model inputs and outputs in a Frenet frame defined by lane centreline sequences. To this end, we present a general wrapper that leverages a Frenet representation of the scene, and that can be applied to SotA models without changing their architecture. We demonstrate the effectiveness of this approach in a comprehensive benchmark using two SotA motion prediction models. Our experiments show that this reduces the off-road rate on challenging scenarios by more than 90% without sacrificing average performance.</p></details> |  |
| **[WROOM: An Autonomous Driving Approach for Off-Road Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.08855v1)** | 2024-04-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Off-road navigation is a challenging problem both at the planning level to get a smooth trajectory and at the control level to avoid flipping over, hitting obstacles, or getting stuck at a rough patch. There have been several recent works using classical approaches involving depth map prediction followed by smooth trajectory planning and using a controller to track it. We design an end-to-end reinforcement learning (RL) system for an autonomous vehicle in off-road environments using a custom-designed simulator in the Unity game engine. We warm-start the agent by imitating a rule-based controller and utilize Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) to improve the policy based on a reward that incorporates Control Barrier Functions (CBF), facilitating the agent's ability to generalize effectively to real-world scenarios. The training involves agents concurrently undergoing domain-randomized trials in various environments. We also propose a novel simulation environment to replicate off-road driving scenarios and deploy our proposed approach on a real buggy RC car. Videos and additional results: https://sites.google.com/view/wroom-utd/home</p></details> |  |

### Off-Road Navigation and Path Planning
> Navigation, path planning, motion planning, and trajectory generation in off-road or cross-country environments.

| **[Sideslip-Conditioned Drift Trajectory Planning with Flow Matching and Control Barrier Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22927v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Drifting, the deliberate control of a vehicle at large sideslip angles, is relevant to both motorsport and recovery from unexpected oversteer. Existing autonomous drifting methods typically determine drift intensity through predefined reference trajectories, optimization objectives, or learned policy rewards, rather than accepting a desired sideslip angle as an explicit input. We propose, to the best of our knowledge, the first generative drift trajectory planner explicitly conditioned on a requested peak sideslip angle. A conditional flow matching model is trained on demonstrated drift laps with hindsight-derived sideslip labels and refined through reinforcement fine-tuning. A control barrier function (CBF) correction is then applied to satisfy model-based road, tire friction, and actuator constraints at the planning waypoints. In closed-loop experiments in the racing simulator Assetto Corsa, a nonlinear model predictive controller tracks the generated plans for six requested sideslip angles from 20 deg to 45 deg, with 30 laps (approximately 75 km) per request. The median peak sideslip angle measured across drift-corner passes follows the requested angle, with a mean absolute error of 1.2 deg across the six conditions. Ablation experiments show that reinforcement fine-tuning improves conditioning accuracy, while CBF correction substantially reduces off-road and spin events.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 4 figures, 3 tables. Under review. Demonstration video: https://youtu.be/nskukDD1MFg</p></details> |
| **[AlphaTruck: A Platform for High-Speed Off-Road Autonomy on Unstructured Terrain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19730v1)** | 2026-11-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Progress in autonomous navigation in off-road environments requires not only advances in algorithms, but also robust and capable physical platforms suited to both development and deployment. Today, off-road autonomy is typically developed and tested at one of two extremes of vehicle scale, each with different drawbacks. Full-size vehicles are capable but expensive, require large logistic infrastructure for deployment and maintenance, and expensive when pushed beyond their limits. RC-scale vehicles are small and inexpensive, but limited in the terrain they can traverse and the payload that they can carry, which includes their sensors and compute. We present the AlphaTruck platform, which leverages recent advances in compact sensing and compute to integrate multimodal sensing, embedded CPU-GPU compute, and an onboard autonomy stack for high-speed, off-road navigation, all integrated into a large-scale, 1/5th RC vehicle chassis. AlphaTruck is designed as both a research testbed and a field-deployable platform for commercial and defense missions such as survey, search and rescue, and agriculture, with the capacity to carry task-specific payloads. At 1/5 scale, the autonomy stack, sensors, and batteries all compete for the same mass, power, and cooling budget, so the platform must balance five needs: mobility over rough terrain, rich sensing, onboard computation, payload capacity to carry the autonomy stack while leaving room for task-specific equipment, and practical operation and repair. Thus, AlphaTruck navigates off-road environments autonomously at 6-10 m/s using only onboard sensing and compute.</p></details> |  |
| **[Autonomous Trail Racing with a Bicycle Robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15280v1)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Navigating off-road trails on a mountain bike requires diverse locomotion skills, including aerial maneuvers, cornering, and slip recovery, paired with sophisticated perception for scene understanding. Translating these capabilities to autonomous, two-wheeled robots remains a formidable challenge. We show that autonomous, high-speed trail navigation can be achieved through a single idea: encoding the trail as a semantic layer of the terrain. In simulation, this layer embeds the navigation task into the training environment; during deployment, it is reconstructed onboard from camera and LiDAR data. First, we introduce a unified learning framework that treats local navigation and locomotion as a joint optimization problem, bypassing the twist or pose command interface of classical control hierarchies. With fixed reward weights, the same framework produces both trail racing and agile parkour, shaped only through terrain semantics and episode length. Second, our perception pipeline combines a frozen vision foundation model with a lightweight dual-head decoder that predicts per-pixel terrain semantics and confidence scores, which are fused with LiDAR range measurements into an egocentric semantic elevation map at 30 Hz onboard an NVIDIA AGX Orin. We validate our framework on the Ultra Mobility Vehicle (UMV), a bicycle robot that autonomously raced across all sections of a kilometer-long, unstructured mountain bike trail at speeds exceeding 5 m/s and averaging 3.26 m/s. In simulation, our policy fails up to an order of magnitude less often than hierarchical baselines while driving faster.</p></details> | <details><summary>35 pa...</summary><p>35 pages, 10 figures, 3 tables (incl. appendix with supplementary figures and tables). Submitted to The International Journal of Robotics Research. Project page: https://pages.rai-inst.com/autonomous-trail-racing/. Code: https://github.com/rai-opensource/sem_map</p></details> |
| **[When to Ask for Help: Uncertainty-Triggered Aerial Refinement for Off-Road Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06702v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Heterogeneous air-ground teams can use aerial sensing to address the limited range and viewpoint of ground-vehicle sensors, but deciding when the UGV needs assistance and what information the UAV should collect remains a challenge. This paper presents a planner-conditioned air-ground assistance method that evaluates how uncertain map information affects the UGV's current MPPI rollouts. When navigation remains degraded, we score the same rollouts under resolved map hypotheses, select the map layer with the largest useful improvement, request the corresponding aerial measurement, fuse the returned information, and replan. Unlike approaches driven by fixed support roles, task structure, or uncertainty alone, our method conditions assistance on whether resolving uncertainty improves predicted ground navigation. Across 180 simulation trials on three off-road routes, the method achieves 98.9% goal attainment while reducing mean UAV flight time by 87.7% relative to continuous greedy aerial mapping. Aggregated across the routes, each second of estimated UAV flight time saves 2.94 s of UGV navigation time. Field experiments spanning approximately 2 km of UGV operation demonstrate uncertainty resolution and successful navigation recovery.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://anonymous.4open.science/w/when-to-ask-for-help/</p></details> |
| **[MS-DOTT: A Multi-Modal Dataset for Off-Road Traversability Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02992v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous robots are gaining traction in off-road environments, driven by their rising demand in agriculture, defense, and search-and-rescue operations. However, these environments are inherently unpredictable and navigation remains a formidable challenge given the complex environmental conditions including challenging terrain and varying illumination. Off-road autonomy requires robust perception across multiple spatial scales and imaging modalities, yet most sensing setups struggle to capture both far-field environmental context and near-field terrain detail simultaneously across various illumination conditions. To facilitate development of robust off-road perception and traversability estimation systems, we propose MS-DOTT (Multi-Sensor Dataset for Off-road Terrain Traversability) a comprehensive multi-modal off-road dataset with long-range 3D sensing and short-range, fine-grained perception to facilitate high-fidelity terrain understanding by combining proprioceptive signals for tracking vehicle dynamics with exteroceptive imaging and pointcloud data. The dataset includes trajectories recorded across nominal lighting, low-light, and no-light conditions with diverse 2D and 3D sensing for both long-range large-scale and short-range high-resolution sensing, as well as 4D RADAR measurements that provide velocity estimates for detected pointclouds. We demonstrate the utility of the dataset by adapting a SOTA multi-sensor fusion method for the purpose of traversability estimation. This dataset will aid the robotics community in advancing research and development of methods for traversability estimation, off-road perception and multi-sensor fusion in unstructured environments.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to RAAI Conference 2026</p></details> |
| **[A Cross-Country Evaluation of Sentiment Toward Digital Payment Systems in Africa](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11566v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Digital payment systems are a cornerstone of African consumer finance, including money transfer applications, mobile money, cryptocurrencies, stablecoins, and central bank digital currencies (CBDCs). While many studies explore how people use digital payment systems (in Africa and beyond), little is known about how people choose and reason about categories of payment systems. We conducted qualitative interviews in three African countries--Nigeria, Tanzania, and Zimbabwe--to understand how and why people use various payment systems. Our study suggests that users navigate accounts and payments on multiple payment systems through a complex selection process based on perceived tradeoffs between utility, privacy, and security. Many users trust government issuers to protect them from scams, but they do not trust those same institutions to build reliable products or prioritize customer satisfaction. Our findings suggest compelling directions for regulators and the research community to design systems that balance users' trust and utility needs.</p></details> |  |
| **[WildOS: Open-Vocabulary Object Search in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.19308v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous navigation in complex, unstructured outdoor environments requires robots to operate over long ranges without prior maps and limited depth sensing. In such settings, relying solely on geometric frontiers for exploration is often insufficient. In such settings, the ability to reason semantically about where to go and what is safe to traverse is crucial for robust, efficient exploration. This work presents WildOS, a unified system for long-range, open-vocabulary object search that combines safe geometric exploration with semantic visual reasoning. WildOS builds a sparse navigation graph to maintain spatial memory, while utilizing a foundation-model-based vision module, ExploRFM, to score frontier nodes of the graph. ExploRFM simultaneously predicts traversability, visual frontiers, and object similarity in image space, enabling real-time, onboard semantic navigation tasks. The resulting vision-scored graph enables the robot to explore semantically meaningful directions while ensuring geometric safety. Furthermore, we introduce a particle-filter-based method for coarse localization of the open-vocabulary target query, that estimates candidate goal positions beyond the robot's immediate depth horizon, enabling effective planning toward distant goals. Extensive closed-loop field experiments across diverse off-road and urban terrains demonstrate that WildOS enables robust navigation, significantly outperforming purely geometric and purely vision-based baselines in both efficiency and autonomy. Our results highlight the potential of vision foundation models to drive open-world robotic behaviors that are both semantically informed and geometrically grounded. Project Page: https://leggedrobotics.github.io/wildos/</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published at IEEE Transactions on Field Robotics</p></details> |
| **[OWLS: Open-world Language-conditioned Speed Fields for Zero-Shot Rover Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32596v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Semantics can provide a proxy for physical traversability. Terrain that looks like loose sand or a rock field is likely to have similar physical properties. Open-vocabulary vision-language models (VLMs) enable this physical reasoning over terrain outside any fixed label set. We present Open-world Language-conditioned Speed Fields (OWLS), which plans directly over the dense feature map of a VLM. Prompted with a description of traversable terrain, the VLM scores every pixel of a single camera image; OWLS projects these scores onto the ground, adapts them to the platform with two morphological operations, and uses the result as the speed field of a minimum-time planner, so terrain appearance and obstacle clearance shape one trajectory. OWLS needs no task-specific training, no terrain annotation, no persistent map, and no exteroceptive sensor beyond the camera. We evaluate it on a physical rover indoors and off-road, on simulated Mars and Moon rovers over 200 procedurally generated terrains, and on real planetary imagery. Project page: https://chahyon-ku.github.io/owls/</p></details> | 8 pages. 6 figures |
| **[Value Function-Guided MPPI for Hierarchical Planning and Control in Off-Road Autonomous Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38731v1)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous navigation in off-road terrain is commonly achieved using hierarchical control and planning architectures that rely on explicit cost maps and one-way information flow from a high-level planner to a low-level controller, decoupling planning from the true execution cost experienced during control. We propose a hierarchical planning and control framework in which information flows bidirectionally, allowing the low-level controller to guide trajectory selection at the planning level. The highest level of the hierarchy is a Model Predictive Path Integral (MPPI) control-style planner that determines optimal trajectories and passes these waypoints to a pretrained low-level reinforcement learning (RL) controller. Rather than drawing from an explicit cost map, our MPPI planner leverages the value function of the RL controller as an implicit cost representation. In this formulation, MPPI serves as a trajectory exploration mechanism, while the low-level controller's value function guides the search. To account for the distribution shift seen by the value function between training and evaluation, we propose an online policy evaluation process that adapts the value function to unseen driving experiences, and we demonstrate this adaptation in unseen off-road simulation environments. We evaluate our approach in a high-fidelity off-road driving simulator across a range of challenging terrain conditions and compare its performance against a baseline MPPI controller. The results demonstrate that our method achieves safer and more effective navigation to goal locations while eliminating the need for explicit cost maps and enabling tighter coupling between planning and control.</p></details> |  |
| **[DGT-Map: Directional Global Traversability Mapping Utilizing Multi-Task Learning for Heterogeneous Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30461v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Off-road traversability is direction-dependent and vehicle specific, yet most global maps assign a single isotropic cost to each location. Existing learned estimators are also commonly trained independently for each vehicle; this preserves vehicle-specific behavior but prevents vehicles from sharing common terrain representations. DGT-MAP addresses both limitations through a self-supervised framework that learns global, directional, and vehicle-conditioned traversability costmaps from RGB-D observations and locomotion signals. A shared multi-task backbone learns common terrain features across training vehicles while vehicle-specific prediction heads preserve platform-dependent responses. At inference, DGT-MAP produces a heading-indexed costmap that can be used by a direction-aware planner. We evaluate DGT-MAP in simulation by integrating it into a Hybrid A* navigation stack and measuring downstream task success on challenging terrains, including slopes that are traversable downhill but not uphill and a ridge obstacle that is traversable by some vehicles, but not by others. Across evaluated tasks, DGT-MAP achieves the highest or tied-highest navigation success rate when compared against geometric, binary, and learned direction-agnostic baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[TravSUITE: Traversability via Self-Supervised, Uncertainty-Aware IRL and Terrain Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32130v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Traversability analysis in off-road settings remains a fundamental challenge for mobile robots. Key difficulties include constructing an accurate and expressive local map from multimodal sensor data, and using the map to design traversability rules that yield desirable navigation behavior. Importantly, this system must be resilient to the limited sensing regime brought about by complex environments and high speeds. In this paper, we present TravSUITE, a traversability system suitable for high-speed navigation in off-road environments. TravSUITE consists of two major components: 1) an efficient voxel mapper that leverages visual foundation models (VFMs) to build a rich geometric-semantic local map from streams of on-board sensor data, and 2) a unified neural network that jointly predicts traversability-relevant quantities in bird's eye view (BEV), including geometry, semantics, speed and cost. Our training strategy is entirely annotation-free and self-supervised, leveraging tasks such as map inpainting and inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) to learn both map representations and traversability. We perform a thorough ablation study and comparison to state-of-the-art approaches, and the results indicate that cost learning and auxiliary inpainting each contribute significantly to planning quality, and their combination is critical for achieving state-of-the-art performance in off-road path planning. We also design a simple risk adaptation mechanism to leverage our method's uncertainty estimates at deploy-time, and demonstrate that a combination of inpainting and risk estimation can result in 80% fewer navigation errors and 5% faster autonomous traversal speeds in real-world hardware experiments.</p></details> |  |
| **[ProTrav: Probabilistic Traversability with Elevation Uncertainty Propagation for Risk-aware Off-road Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30839v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reliable traversability estimation is essential for autonomous navigation on unstructured terrain. State-of-the-art approaches typically evaluate traversability from deterministic terrain elevation, thereby overlooking spatial correlations in the terrain and failing to account for how elevation uncertainty propagates through nonlinear traversability mappings. We propose ProTrav, a probabilistic terrain representation that uses function-space sampling to generate spatially correlated terrain realizations and propagate elevation uncertainty to traversability cost. First, a Sparse Variational Gaussian Process (SVGP) reconstructs a probabilistic elevation field from observations. Then, posterior function-space sampling generates spatially correlated terrain realizations, preserving spatial dependencies between neighboring locations while propagating elevation uncertainty through geometric features into traversability cost. Consequently, a distribution of traversability cost at arbitrary location is established, where the mean and standard deviation quantify the expected traversability cost and associated uncertainty, respectively. A risk term is defined by moment-based Upper Confidence Bound (UCB) and incorporated into a Model Predictive Path Integral (MPPI) controller for risk-aware navigation. Simulation experiments demonstrate navigation success rates exceeding 90\% on different terrains, compared with below 40\% for a baseline, while reducing attitude variations. Furthermore, the proposed approach is validated on a robot traversing real-world off-road terrain.</p></details> | 9 pages, 7 figures |
| **[PATH: Preference-Aware Traversability Estimation for Robotic Teleoperation in Off-Road Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30605v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Traversability estimation is critical for ground vehicle teleoperation, yet existing methods often neglect the human operator's preferences for speed and risk tolerance. ### Off-Road Freespace and Traversability
> Freespace detection, terrain traversability estimation, and drivable-area reasoning for unstructured terrain.

| **[RESCUE Jumper: A Centimeter-Scale Rolling and Jumping Robot for Search and Rescue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23531v2)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Centimeter-scale robots are promising for urban search and rescue because they can enter confined spaces that are inaccessible or unsafe for humans. The RESCUE Jumper uses its two actuators for both rolling mode and jumping by reversing the direction of wheel motion. Forward wheel motion enables rolling, while reverse wheel motion winds a spring steel tail to store elastic potential energy and release it for jumping. The RESCUE Jumper varies tail winding speed to control jump direction, transitioning from backward to forward jumps as winding speed increases from 0.75 rad/s to 8.73 rad/s. The launch angle of the jumps ranges from 45 degree to -11 degree with respect to the vertical axis, reaching a maximum height of 9.2 cm and a maximum lateral displacement of 11.2 cm. We model the winding speed-dependent jumping behavior in three phases: tail release to determine the contact point and robot orientation, tail straightening until liftoff, and ballistic flight to predict jump height and lateral displacement. Finally, qualitative tests highlight the robot's locomotion in cluttered, real-world environments. DOI: 10.1109/LRA.2025.3619687</p></details> |

### Unstructured Environment Autonomous Navigation
> Autonomous navigation and planning in rough, complex, or weakly structured outdoor environments.

| **[Bridging vision-language reasoning and learned control for legged navigation in the wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25215v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous navigation in diverse real-world environments remains one of the greatest challenges in robotics. It demands both robust low-level motion control to handle diverse terrain and high-level reasoning to interpret environmental cues. We present ROAM (Robust Open-world Autonomous Mobility), a system that enables legged robots to perform kilometer-scale navigation in complex alpine and urban settings. ROAM combines the world knowledge of a Vision-Language Model (VLM), pre-trained on internet-scale data, with robust locomotion and local planning policies learned in simulation via reinforcement learning (RL). The VLM provides common-sense reasoning and semantic interpretation of the scene, assessing risks, interpreting signs, and producing semantic labels with associated navigation costs. An open-vocabulary segmentation module converts these labels into a dense, real-time risk map, which conditions two pre-trained RL policies responsible for agile locomotion and local navigation, effectively coupling high-level VLM reasoning with low-level physical control. Real-world experiments showed the system's ability to autonomously navigate a wide range of complex real-world environments. The robot completes kilometer-long hikes with minimal intervention, interprets signs to avoid danger, and evaluates its environment to identify safe areas on which to step. This work represents a step toward the reliable deployment of systems in unstructured environments. However, it also underscores the challenges that remain in VLM reliability and spatial reasoning, indicating that foundational models are a crucial component but not yet a complete solution for navigation in the wild.</p></details> | <details><summary>42 pa...</summary><p>42 pages, 6 figures, 15 supplementary figures. To address the hallenge, we propose HEHA (Hierarchical Exploration with Heterogeneous Agents) using a recent hierarchical method that decomposes the exploration into global planning and local planning. Additionally, the local planner in HEHA also considers heterogeneity to avoid repeated and duplicated exploration among the robots. The experimental results show, in the presence of heterogeneous traversability, our RCH can solve mHPP with up to 47% smaller makespan than several baselines, and HEHA can reduce the exploration time by up to 30% compared with existing approaches.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by T-RO. 16 Figures</p></details> |

Using this framework, this paper presents a high-quality, large-scale dataset consisting of 6 deformable objects, totaling 110 minutes of trajectory data. Project page: https://roahmlab.github.io/trackDeform3D-core-tracking/</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the IEEE/RSJ International Conference on Intelligent Robots and Systems (IROS), 2026</p></details> |
| **[Linear Twisting Sidewinding Gaits for Twistable Snake Robot Motion Planning on Complex Terrains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29461v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sidewinding is an efficient gait for snake robots to locomote on various terrains, by undulating the backbone curve and shifting the contact segments along it. This hypothesis has recently been verified by the rolling vector approach, which locomotes the robot like a differential-drive car. | **[ESCAPE: Egocentric Sensing for Collision Avoidance and Policy Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23943v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Motion planning in unstructured environments is a bottleneck for robot deployment. Results demonstrate that CMoE enables the robot to traverse continuous steps up to 20 cm high and gaps up to 80 cm wide, while achieving robust and natural gait across diverse mixed terrains, surpassing the limits of existing methods. | **[Physics-Guided Robotic Radiation Source Localization along Arbitrary Measurement Paths in Unstructured Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27624v2)** | 2026-09-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Using robots to estimate the location of the radiation source is an effective way to improve efficiency and safety. | **[MANTLE: A Framework for Adaptive In-Situ Planetary Perception Using a Modular Uplink Principle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28724v1)** | 2026-08-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Planetary surface exploration missions rely increasingly on autonomous robotic platforms capable of interpreting complex terrain to ensure safe navigation, enable targeted science, and improve operational efficiency, as demonstrated across past Mars missions from Viking through Perseverance. This work demonstrates two such high-impact capabilities, terrain classification and boulder segmentation, as an initial realization of a framework built to support many more over a mission's lifetime. While classical algorithms guaranteed collision-free paths, their excessive path lengths and processing times render them less suitable for dynamic underwater operations. A modular 6x12 sampling system, controlled by distributed micro-ROS nodes, provides deterministic actuation, fault isolation, and rapid module replacement, achieving spatial coverage beyond previously reported USV-based samplers. Field trials in Achocalla Lagoon (La Paz, Bolivia) demonstrated 87% waypoint accuracy, stable autonomous navigation, and accurate physicochemical measurements (temperature, pH, conductivity, total dissolved solids) comparable to manually collected references. These results demonstrate that the platform enables reliable high-resolution sampling and autonomous mission execution, providing a scalable solution for aquatic monitoring in remote environments.</p></details> |  |
| **[Traversability Aware Autonomous Navigation for Multi-Modal Mobility Morphobot (M4)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.11876v1)** | 2025-12-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous navigation in unstructured environments requires robots to assess terrain difficulty in real-time and plan paths that balance efficiency with safety. This thesis presents a traversability-aware navigation framework for the M4 robot platform that uses learned terrain analysis to generate energy-efficient paths avoiding difficult terrain.Our approach uses FAST-LIO for real-time localization, generating 2.5D elevation maps from LiDAR point clouds. A CNN-based model processes these elevation maps to estimate traversability scores, which are converted into navigation costs for path planning. A custom A* planner incorporates these costs alongside geometric distance and energy consumption to find paths that trade modest distance increases for substantial terrain quality improvements. Before system development, a platform-agnostic study compared LiDAR-based and camera-based SLAM using OptiTrack ground truth. Point cloud comparison through ICP alignment and cloud-to-mesh distance analysis demonstrated that LiDAR-based mapping achieves centimeter-level precision essential for elevation mapping, while camera-based approaches exhibited significantly higher geometric error. These findings directly resulted in the selection of LiDAR as the primary sensor to generate elevation maps. The complete pipeline integrates FAST-LIO localization, GPU-accelerated elevation mapping, CNN-based traversability estimation, and Nav2 navigation with a custom traversability-aware planner. Experimental results demonstrate that the system successfully avoids low traversability regions and accepts a few longer paths to achieve a reduction in terrain cost. This work establishes a foundation for intelligent terrain-aware navigation applicable to multi-modal robotic platforms.</p></details> | Master's thesis |
| **[Vision-Guided Optic Flow Navigation for Small Lunar Missions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.17720v1)** | 2025-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Private lunar missions are faced with the challenge of robust autonomous navigation while operating under stringent constraints on mass, power, and computational resources. This work proposes a motion-field inversion framework that uses optical flow and rangefinder-based depth estimation as a lightweight CPU-based solution for egomotion estimation during lunar descent. We extend classical optical flow formulations by integrating them with depth modeling strategies tailored to the geometry for lunar/planetary approach, descent, and landing, specifically, planar and spherical terrain approximations parameterized by a laser rangefinder. Motion field inversion is performed through a least-squares framework, using sparse optical flow features extracted via the pyramidal Lucas-Kanade algorithm. We verify our approach using synthetically generated lunar images over the challenging terrain of the lunar south pole, using CPU budgets compatible with small lunar landers. The results demonstrate accurate velocity estimation from approach to landing, with sub-10% error for complex terrain and on the order of 1% for more typical terrain, as well as performances suitable for real-time applications. This framework shows promise for enabling robust, lightweight on-board navigation for small lunar missions.</p></details> |  |
| **[VLM-SFD: VLM-Assisted Siamese Flow Diffusion Framework for Dual-Arm Cooperative Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13428v2)** | 2025-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dual-arm cooperative manipulation holds great promise for tackling complex real-world tasks that demand seamless coordination and adaptive dynamics. Despite substantial progress in learning-based motion planning, most approaches struggle to generalize across diverse manipulation tasks and adapt to dynamic, unstructured environments, particularly in scenarios involving interactions between two objects such as assembly, tool use, and bimanual grasping. To address these challenges, we introduce a novel VLM-Assisted Siamese Flow Diffusion (VLM-SFD) framework for efficient imitation learning in dual-arm cooperative manipulation. The proposed VLM-SFD framework exhibits outstanding adaptability, significantly enhancing the ability to rapidly adapt and generalize to diverse real-world tasks from only a minimal number of human demonstrations. Specifically, we propose a Siamese Flow Diffusion Network (SFDNet) employs a dual-encoder-decoder Siamese architecture to embed two target objects into a shared latent space, while a diffusion-based conditioning process - conditioned by task instructions - generates two-stream object-centric motion flows that guide dual-arm coordination. We further design a dynamic task assignment strategy that seamlessly maps the predicted 2D motion flows into 3D space and incorporates a pre-trained vision-language model (VLM) to adaptively assign the optimal motion to each robotic arm over time. Experiments validate the effectiveness of the proposed method, demonstrating its ability to generalize to diverse manipulation tasks while maintaining high efficiency and adaptability. The code and demo videos are publicly available on our project website https://sites.google.com/view/vlm-sfd/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by IEEE RA-L</p></details> |
| **[DR. Nav: Semantic-Geometric Representations for Proactive Dead-End Recovery and Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.12778v1)** | 2025-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present DR. Nav (Dead-End Recovery-aware Navigation), a novel approach to autonomous navigation in scenarios where dead-end detection and recovery are critical, particularly in unstructured environments where robots must handle corners, vegetation occlusions, and blocked junctions. DR. Nav introduces a proactive strategy for navigation in unmapped environments without prior assumptions. Our method unifies dead-end prediction and recovery by generating a single, continuous, real-time semantic cost map. Specifically, DR. Nav leverages cross-modal RGB-LiDAR fusion with attention-based filtering to estimate per-cell dead-end likelihoods and recovery points, which are continuously updated through Bayesian inference to enhance robustness. Unlike prior mapping methods that only encode traversability, DR. Nav explicitly incorporates recovery-aware risk into the navigation cost map, enabling robots to anticipate unsafe regions and plan safer alternative trajectories. We evaluate DR. Nav across multiple dense indoor and outdoor scenarios and demonstrate an increase of 83.33% in accuracy in detection, a 52.4% reduction in time-to-goal (path efficiency), compared to state-of-the-art planners such as DWA, MPPI, and Nav2 DWB. Furthermore, the dead-end classifier functions</p></details> |  |
| **[Local Path Planning with Dynamic Obstacle Avoidance in Unstructured Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.07927v1)** | 2025-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Obstacle avoidance and path planning are essential for guiding unmanned ground vehicles (UGVs) through environments that are densely populated with dynamic obstacles. This paper develops a novel approach that combines tangentbased path planning and extrapolation methods to create a new decision-making algorithm for local path planning. In the assumed scenario, a UGV has a prior knowledge of its initial and target points within the dynamic environment. A global path has already been computed, and the robot is provided with waypoints along this path. As the UGV travels between these waypoints, the algorithm aims to avoid collisions with dynamic obstacles. These obstacles follow polynomial trajectories, with their initial positions randomized in the local map and velocities randomized between O and the allowable physical velocity limit of the robot, along with some random accelerations. The developed algorithm is tested in several scenarios where many dynamic obstacles move randomly in the environment. Simulation results show the effectiveness of the proposed local path planning strategy by gradually generating a collision free path which allows the robot to navigate safely between initial and the target locations.</p></details> |  |
| **[Keeping it Local, Tiny and Real: Automated Report Generation on Edge Computing Devices for Mechatronic-Based Cognitive Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.02507v1)** | 2025-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advancements in Deep Learning enable hardware-based cognitive systems, that is, mechatronic systems in general and robotics in particular with integrated Artificial Intelligence, to interact with dynamic and unstructured environments. While the results are impressive, the application of such systems to critical tasks like autonomous driving as well as service and care robotics necessitate the evaluation of large amount of heterogeneous data. Automated report generation for Mobile Robotics can play a crucial role in facilitating the evaluation and acceptance of such systems in various domains. In this paper, we propose a pipeline for generating automated reports in natural language utilizing various multi-modal sensors that solely relies on local models capable of being deployed on edge computing devices, thus preserving the privacy of all actors involved and eliminating the need for external services. In particular, we evaluate our implementation on a diverse dataset spanning multiple domains including indoor, outdoor and urban environments, providing quantitative as well as qualitative evaluation results. Various generated example reports and other supplementary materials are available via a public repository.</p></details> | <details><summary>6 pag...</summary><p>6 pages, 4 figures, 1 table; accepted for MECATRONICS-REM 2025 International Conference, PARIS, FRANCE December 3-5 2025</p></details> |

### VLA for Autonomous Driving
> Vision-language-action models and action generation methods for autonomous driving.

| **[AlpaSafe: Risk-Aware Trajectory Selection for Vision-Language-Action Models in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28509v1)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reasoning-based driving vision-language-action (VLA) models propose multiple candidate trajectories at each decision, yet only one is executed. Standard evaluation scores the best candidate, assuming an oracle selector that deployment lacks, and leaves the risk of the executed candidate unexamined. Existing online evaluators typically close this gap with a purpose-trained scene or world model, often rolling out each candidate through it at inference time. We introduce AlpaSafe, a risk-aware trajectory selector that reuses the scene representation a frozen driving VLA computes while proposing its candidates. AlpaSafe reads the last hidden token of the VLA's prompt prefill. A lightweight head, trained on the closed-loop simulation outcomes of the candidates, fuses this token with each candidate trajectory and predicts a relative score and collision/offroad logits. A fixed rule removes candidates dominated on the two risk logits and selects the highest-scoring candidate among those remaining, adding neither rollouts nor VLA training. Experiments on 525 AlpaSim scenes show that AlpaSafe outperforms Alpamayo's default first-sample rule and uniform random selection in selected regret and top-1 agreement, and reduces the collision-at-fault or offroad rate from 6.41% and 6.10% to 4.93%. The selector has 1.7M parameters and takes 0.75 ms per decision.</p></details> |  |
| **[Intervention-Robust VLA: Diverse Demonstrations for Robot Deployment with Safety Filters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28289v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) have shown strong potential for learning complex robotic behaviors from demonstrations. However, learning safe behavior directly from data requires extensive coverage of safety-critical situations, which is often impractical in real-world settings. Test-time safety filters provide an alternative by correcting unsafe actions during deployment, but these interventions can drive the robot into states poorly covered by the training data, thereby degrading task performance. We introduce InteR-VLA, an intervention-robust framework for VLAs that leverages diverse task-completing demonstrations to broaden task-relevant state coverage and maintain competence under safety interventions. By better aligning the training distribution with safety-filtered deployment under the same data budget, our approach mitigates intervention-induced distribution shift while preserving task performance. Real-world experiments across in-distribution and out-of-distribution settings show that InteR-VLA maintains safety, increases Safe Success Rate up to 90% from 0% for nominal policies, and consistently outperforms nominal-data training on both evaluated VLAs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond a Single Human Demonstration: Learning from Scored Trajectory Alternatives for End-to-End Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27515v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human driving logs record only one trajectory per scene, leaving other feasible driving choices without explicit trajectory targets or quality labels. This limits supervision for end-to-end planners that generate and refine multiple candidate trajectories. Meanwhile, conditioning refinement on a large vision-language model adds substantial online inference cost. We propose DriveSTA, which extends autoregressive proposal generation and proposal-conditioned residual flow refinement with scored trajectory alternatives. For each training scene, we generate alternatives offline through smooth deformations of the recorded trajectory and evaluate their simulated execution against logged traffic. Diverse, high-quality alternatives supervise distinct proposals and define residual targets for quality-guided denoising, while candidates spanning the full score range train a scorer to assess driving quality. A shared joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA), adapted through masked video-feature prediction, encodes observed frames once and provides temporal context for both refinement and scoring. On NAVSIM v1 Navtest, DriveSTA ranks first among the compared planners trained on NAVSIM data alone. With expanded training data, DriveSTA-Scale achieves state-of-the-art results among the compared methods on Navtest and closed-loop HUGSIM, while reducing mean planner forward-pass latency by 73% relative to ChainFlow-VLA in matched evaluations.</p></details> | <details><summary>21 pa...</summary><p>21 pages, 9 figures, including supplementary material. Project page: https://rtwotwo.github.io/DriveSTA</p></details> |
| **[PhyDEX: A Benchmark for Physical Reasoning in Vision-Language-Action Models via Dynamic Extrinsic Dexterity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26565v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have advanced rapidly in language-conditioned robotic manipulation, yet existing benchmarks mainly evaluate gripper--object contact and largely ignore how objects interact with the environment through gravity, friction, inertia, and mass distribution. As a result, it remains unclear whether current policies truly understand physics or merely interpolate from their training data. We address this gap through the lens of dynamic extrinsic dexterity and introduce PhyDEX, a throwing benchmark in which a robot must infer an object's mass, center of mass, and friction to send it to a destination beyond its kinematic workspace. PhyDEX contains 35K trajectories (143 hours) across nine tasks and 100 scenes, covering uniform, irregular, and dynamic-mass objects with static, multiple, and moving destinations. All data are generated by a scalable, fully automatic pipeline that combines grasp generation, motion planning, and closed-loop throw optimization under randomized physical properties. We evaluate three representative VLAs, GR00T N1.7, DynamicVLA, and ForceVLA, and find that all achieve success rates below 3\%. Our analysis reveals two key bottlenecks: limited robustness of pretrained models, which causes most failures before the throw, and physical blindness, as providing ground-truth physical properties roughly doubles success. Experiments on a humanoid robot and in the real world show the same failure patterns. We hope PhyDEX will drive the development of physically grounded robot policies.</p></details> |  |
| **[Composable Driving Style for End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14802v2)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>End-to-end driving policies can achieve strong task performance while offering limited control over their driving style. Driving style describes how a vehicle accelerates, brakes, and maintains distance. Policies trained without explicit preference conditioning lack a direct mechanism for adjusting these behaviors at inference. We propose YOUDrive, a vision--language--action planner for continuous and composable driving-style control. Each teacher's behavior is distilled into a task vector, a learned parameter update that induces a style direction in the planner's representation. These directions can be scaled and combined at inference without retraining the backbone. Conditioned on these directions, a flow-matching decoder models a multimodal trajectory distribution and generates kinematically feasible motion alternatives. We introduce the Style Alignment Score (SAS) to describe human-referenced kinematic behavior after safety and task gating. On NAVSIM navtest, a single coefficient monotonically controls assertiveness across six teacher-derived personas, while PDMS remains above 0.90 across the tested individual and blended configurations, reaching 0.954. Further analyses reveal systematic policy-specific kinematics after controlling for scene context, whereas the evaluated discrete style labels provide limited additional explanatory power. | **[GroundingPI: A Grounding Foundation Model towards Physical Intelligence with Visual Primitives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39601v3)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Precise grounding matters. It specifies which object is the target and where that object is, even in clutter and for tiny objects, and it has to be fast enough for closed-loop control. Across RoboTwin 2.0 and RoboCasa-GR1, it outperforms all evaluated backbones in all four out-of-distribution settings, with relative gains of up to 24.8% over the strongest baseline. Project page: https://groundingpi.github.io/ Code: https://github.com/groundingpi/GroundingPI Model: https://huggingface.co/GroundingPI/GroundingPI</p></details> |
| **[Ego2X: Contact-aware Transfer from Egocentric Human Data to Any Robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24138v2)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied models depend on real interaction data, yet collecting it on physical robots is slow and labor-intensive. Thus, we present Ego2X, an object-centric framework that converts monocular egocentric video into physically executable robot data across dexterous hands and parallel grippers. Ego2X centers on CORE (Contact-centric Object-interaction REpresentation), which records each contact on the object by its surface anchor, as well as relative force, grasp mode, and interaction state. Throughout the pipeline, CORE corrects hand-object drift via object-relative constraints, steers inverse kinematics toward contacts the robot must realize, and preserves held objects during arm erasure and robot compositing. On HOT3D, contact-driven correction reduces in-contact hand depth error by 33.7%, while on EgoDex, contact-driven retargeting realizes 62.9% of annotated contacts, surpassing the official HuRo pipeline (27.9%). Across LIBERO, CALVIN, WidowX, and Google Robot, one-knob-at-a-time ablations show that training dynamics are the largest measured driver, producing performance swings of $Δ$$\approx$54--94\%, while task configuration and architecture have smaller effects under matched settings. | **[TuringPAI: A Foundation for On-Device Physical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25474v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Physical AI requires models that understand spatial relationships and dynamic environments and translate observations into actions under limited onboard compute. | **[Urban State Representation for Autonomous Mobility: A Survey of Multimodal Perception, Architectures, Datasets, and Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28129v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cities are sensed by many platforms at once: on-board cameras, LiDAR and radar on autonomous vehicles, roadside units at intersections, drones and satellites overhead, and the GNSS traces of millions of trips. We survey this fragmented landscape through a single lens, the Urban State Representation (USR): a georeferenced, layered, time-varying, uncertainty-aware state that any sensing platform can write to and any downstream task can read from. This survey is an entry point for computer vision, autonomous driving, transportation, and urban computing researchers; the paper list, datasets, and USR-5 code are at https://github.com/binayksingh/Awesome-Urban-State-Representation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the UrbanState Workshop at WACV 2027. 8 pages + references + 5-page supplementary appendix. | **[SHAPE: Discrete Supervision and Parallel Continuous Streams for Learning Dexterous Manipulation from Egocentric Human Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24585v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Egocentric human recordings are far cheaper than dexterous robot demonstrations, promising stronger pretraining and direct skill learning without task-specific robot data. Yet humans and robots differ in morphology and kinematics, so human recordings lack the joint positions and actuator commands that supervise robot policies, providing only retargeted end-effector (EEF) actions. On a bimanual robot with 22-DoF hands, a matched study shows that discrete supervision helps learn from human data, driving backbone gradients and shaping action-informative representations across domains. Under standard control pipelines at deployment, direct joint readout excels at fine-grained control with robot demonstrations, scoring 0.80 across six tasks against 0.63 for π0.5, whereas IK-based EEF readout excels at human-to-robot transfer. | **[SuperGlance: Driving at a Glance with a Vision-Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19138v2)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous driving is turning to vision-language-action models (VLAs). They answer and plan by decoding, one step at a time. Each step is another forward pass, so a plan can take over a second of GPU time. Embodied AI often pairs such a slow, reasoning System 2 with a fast System 1 that acts. Human drivers plan at a glance, with System 1. Faster designs usually put System 1 outside the vision-language model (VLM), in a planner without its abilities. Yet before a VLM writes its first token, it has already read the scene into one hidden state. We show that, once trained, this hidden state is enough to drive and to answer questions about what the model sees. SuperGlance reads it to turn a decoder-only VLM into an encoder-style driving policy. Its linear head scores a fixed set of 5,273 candidate trajectories. With the set fixed, rewards are computed offline, so supervised targets can be reward-weighted and reinforcement learning maximizes the exact expected reward. Before and during policy training, multiple-choice training keeps the VLM's visual abilities in one pass, so the policy still answers questions about driving scenes. Training questions are built against shortcuts. Each must be answered right in two option orders. Against drift, a KL term keeps each stage near its starting model. One pass offers VLAs a more plausible path to System 1. SuperGlance answers visual multiple-choice questions as well as its base VLM does by decoding. It plans in 84 ms of GPU time, six times faster than Qwen-Drive-1.0, and at least as well on NAVSIM, PAI-AV and WOD-E2E. In AlpaSim closed loop, km per at-fault incident holds for both planners up to a 300-ms delay and drops beyond. With GPU time charged as delay, SuperGlance's lead over Qwen-Drive-1.0 grows from 2.8 to 3.9 times. Delayed by 1.2 s, our model falls below Qwen-Drive-1.0 at its own 518 ms.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 10 figures, 20 tables. Project page: https://guobapei.github.io/SuperGlance/ ; code: https://github.com/GuobaPei/SuperGlance</p></details> |
| **[ReactBench-AD: Do Driving VLAs and World-Action Models React to Imminent Hazards in Their Path?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20011v2)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) and World-Action Models (WAMs) have emerged in autonomous driving with the promise of strong generalization to long-tail scenarios. Although these models achieve state-of-the-art results on autonomous driving benchmarks, most existing benchmarks are dominated by nominal scenarios and therefore cannot test this promise. To test one such long-tail capability that is critical for safety, we introduce ReactBench-AD, a benchmark that measures how driving models react to objects that appear in the ego vehicle's path. Built on real NAVSIM and PhysicalAI-Autonomous-Vehicles scenes, ReactBench-AD composites a crossing pedestrian, a standing pedestrian, or a stationary car consistently across all camera views and history frames. Each edited scene thus forms an exact, pixel-level counterfactual pair with its original, which allows us to isolate the causal effect of the inserted object on the predicted trajectory. Evaluating four VLAs and two WAMs, we find that they react weakly, often barely at all. Finally, we test how easily one of the evaluated VLAs, ReCogDrive, can be taught to brake hard for imminent hazards: a short fine-tuning stage on edited scenes substantially improves its reactions, with the learned behavior generalizing to unseen objects and fully real scenes, at minimal cost to nominal driving. | **[Learning Agent-Centric Vision-Language-Action World Models for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22880v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent progress in integrating vision-language-action (VLA) models with world modeling has significantly advanced end-to-end autonomous driving by allowing systems to reason about imagined futures instead of merely reacting to current observations. However, most world VLA models generate future frames at the pixel level with plenty of dense visual tokens. | **[What Can a VLA Learn from the Agent That Calls It? ### VLA for Off-Road Driving
> VLA-style policies and multimodal action reasoning applied to off-road or unstructured driving scenes.

| **[CrossPanel: Exposing the Answer-Evidence Gap in Scientific Figure Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28641v1)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A correct answer to a scientific-figure question can conceal an incorrect account of the experiment. Reading evidence across panels requires keeping each observation attached to the object and conditions that give it meaning. We introduce CrossPanel, a benchmark of 2,650 human-reviewed questions that exposes this answer-evidence gap. Its tasks combine parallel observations or follow dependent queries, while a response audit separates object selection, experimental conditions, cross-panel binding, and local readout. Across 15 vision-language models on 251 diagnostic questions, 8.42%-56.64% of correct answers contain explicit factual errors. The audit distinguishes coherent readings of the wrong target from incorrect readings of the right one. Controlled interventions then test how these failures can be repaired: verified object-condition hints, which withhold the requested measurement, raise Qwen3.5-35B-A3B's target-query success by 22.81 percentage points on 57 questions, while additional image tiles alone yield little improvement. Other models benefit most from combining both forms of assistance. These findings identify experimental attribution as a distinct requirement alongside local perception, and make both measurable beyond final-answer accuracy.</p></details> |  |

To bridge this gap, we propose PreAct-Nav, a training-free agentic navigation framework that equips frozen policies with visual foresight for reasoning before action. Our central idea is to use predicted future views to assess proposed actions in the context of persistent medium-horizon subgoals and observed experience. At its core, a navigation memory module maintains the active subgoal and relevant experience across decisions, translating distant goals into actionable intermediate objectives. To enable visual foresight, a predictive world sandbox uses an action-conditioned world model (AC-WM) to generate future views of proposed movements, providing visual cues for reasoning about their consequences beyond the current observation. A vision-language model (VLM) reasoner interprets these predictions using the subgoal and accumulated experience to retain or revise actions. After execution, real observations are used to assess outcomes, correct inconsistent assumptions, and update memory to continue or reformulate the subgoal. To evaluate the diagnostic accuracy and image navigation behavior of vision-language model (VLM) agents under varying exploration strategies. Materials and Methods. > Visual prompting and reasoning methods that may support planning, navigation, and driving decisions.

| **[Who Says What: Symbolic Trimodal Binding Mechanisms in Audio-Visual LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31193v2)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current Audio-Visual LLMs (AVLLMs) struggle with reasoning over videos featuring multi-speaker dialogues. Motivated by these challenges, we systematically investigate how this trimodal binding is achieved in AVLLMs. Specifically, we identify emergent symbolic trimodal binding mechanisms in AVLLMs that utilize modality-specific symbolic variables. By encoding auditory and visual components into symbolic variables-capturing temporal utterance sequences and spatial entity coordinates, respectively-the model establishes cross-modal linking within this abstract space. Crucially, we reveal that when trimodal binding fails, the breakdown predominantly stems from misaligned audio-visual connections. To overcome this bottleneck, we introduce an audio-visual prompting method utilizing an off-the-shelf Active Speaker Detection (ASD) model. By simply overlaying visual bounding boxes on active speakers, this training-free approach yields immediate performance gains across four conversation-centric benchmarks. Moreover, lightweight fine-tuning of fewer than 300 steps on these ASD-prompted-videos extends these gains to three general AV benchmarks, suggesting the generalizability of our method. We observe that current navigators often bring the drone close to the target and then stop at the wrong place: they reach the target without finding it. This gap is shared by navigators of very different designs, from imitation learners to a fine-tuned 7B vision-language model. A simple test reveals the cause. When we replace the target object in the instruction with another one next to the same landmarks, no navigator goes to it, and most stop where they did before. Navigators learn to reach the landmarks, not to identify the described object. We therefore propose MAP-VP, a plug-and-play module for the last step. After any navigator stops, MAP-VP draws the landmarks named in the instruction onto the drone's current view, using the landmark outlines that the benchmark provides on a map. A fine-tuned 4B vision-language model then locates the target relative to these landmarks. The training data are generated automatically from the benchmark annotations. Neither drawing the landmarks nor fine-tuning helps much alone, but together they are highly effective. Without retraining any navigator, MAP-VP significantly improves all six navigators we test on CityNav. | **[VisionCoach: Reinforcing Grounded Video Reasoning via Visual-Perception Prompting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14659v2)** | 2026-08-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Video reasoning requires models to locate and track question-relevant evidence across frames. | **[FlowInOne:Unifying Multimodal Generation as Image-in, Image-out Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.06757v3)** | 2026-07-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal generation has long been dominated by text-driven pipelines where language dictates vision but cannot reason or create within it. Evaluations on nine recent Video-MLLMs show that FCMBench-Video provides meaningful separation across systems and capabilities: counting is the most duration-sensitive task, Cross-Document Validation and Evidence-Grounded Selection probe higher-level evidence integration, and Visual Prompt Injection provides a complementary robustness dimension. The overall score distribution is broad and approximately bell-shaped, indicating a benchmark that is neither saturated nor dominated by trivial cases. Together, these results position FCMBench-Video as a reproducible benchmark for tracking Video-MLLM progress on document-video understanding and probing capability boundaries in authenticity-sensitive credit-domain applications.</p></details> |  |
| **[Multiple Consistent 2D-3D Mappings for Robust Zero-Shot 3D Visual Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.26261v1)** | 2026-04-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Zero-shot 3D Visual Grounding (3DVG) is a critical capability for open-world embodied AI. Instead of passively relying on noisy 3D segments, MCM-VG enforces 2D-3D consistency across three fundamental dimensions to achieve precise target localization and reliable reasoning. First, a Semantic Alignment module corrects category mismatches via LLM-driven query parsing and coarse-to-fine 2D-3D matching. Second, an Instance Rectification module leverages VLM-guided 2D segmentations to reconstruct missing targets, back-projecting these reliable visual priors to establish accurate 3D geometries. Finally, to eliminate spatial redundancy, a Viewpoint Distillation module clusters 3D camera directions to extract optimal frames. By pairing these optimal RGB frames with Bird's Eye View maps into concise visual prompt sets, we formulate the final target disambiguation as a multiple-choice reasoning task for Vision-Language Models. Extensive evaluations on ScanRefer and Nr3D benchmarks demonstrate that MCM-VG sets a new state-of-the-art for zero-shot 3D visual grounding. | **[EV-CLIP: Efficient Visual Prompt Adaptation for CLIP in Few-shot Action Recognition under Visual Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22595v1)** | 2026-04-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>CLIP has demonstrated strong generalization in visual domains through natural language supervision, even for video action recognition. ### World Models for Autonomous Driving
> World models, future prediction, planning priors, and trajectory reasoning for driving and navigation.

<details><summary>arXiv query</summary><code>(ti:"world model" OR abs:"world model" OR ti:"world models" OR abs:"world models") AND (ti:"autonomous driving" OR abs:"autonomous driving" OR ti:"navigation" OR abs:"navigation" OR ti:"planning" OR abs:"planning" OR ti:"trajectory prediction" OR abs:"trajectory prediction")</code></details>

| **[Persuasion from Within: Model-Based Insider Attacks in Multi-Agent LLM Debate and Consensus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28660v1)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in multi-agent systems for collective decision-making, such as multi-agent debate and consensus games, in which agents exchange messages and update their answers over multiple rounds and a resolution mechanism aggregates them into a final outcome. Most multi-agent LLM frameworks assume that every participant shares the system objective. In practice, however, a malicious agent can join as a legitimate member of the group and persuade the other agents toward a hidden adversarial goal. We formalize such attacks as a sequential decision-making problem in which the insider seeks to degrade the utility of the collective outcome. We propose a model-based insider attack that learns to persuade a group of agents: the insider first learns a world model of the benign agents from offline trajectories and then uses it both to train a persuasion policy and to plan its messages. In two case studies involving a numerical consensus game and a multi-agent debate, the learned insider is the most effective and the stealthiest attack we evaluate, outperforming both prompt-based and trained LLM persuaders from prior work. The attack also transfers to other target models, larger groups, and unseen datasets, and remains effective under existing Byzantine-resilience defenses. The learned insider also enables a new defense: adversarially training benign agents on the attack data largely removes both our attack and the strongest prior persuasion attack. These results show that combining world models with reinforcement learning and planning is an effective route to both attacking and hardening language-based multi-agent systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[HaM-World: Soft-Hamiltonian World Models with Selective Memory for Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05951v3)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World models enable model-based planning through learned latent dynamics, but imagined rollouts become unstable as the planning horizon grows or the dynamics distribution shifts: an unconstrained predictor feeds each predicted state back as input, so the latent distribution contracts until distinct states merge. We argue that this drift and contraction reflect two missing structures in planner-facing latents: a history-conditioned predictive state summarizing past observations and actions, and geometric organization that separates configuration, momentum, and task semantics. We propose HaM-World, a structured world model that decomposes the latent state into a canonical-like (q,p) subspace and a context subspace c, while using Mamba selective state-space memory as the history-conditioned input to the same latent dynamics. The (q,p) subspace evolves through an energy-derived Hamiltonian vector field plus learnable residual/control dynamics, while c captures semantic, dissipative, and non-conservative factors. The planner uses this single latent state for prediction, reward/value estimation, imagined rollouts, and CEM search. On six DeepMind Control Suite tasks, HaM-World ranks first on four and second on the other two, with the highest six-task Avg. AUC (91.9, +10.3% over TD-MPC2). Within the horizons the planner unrolls, it reduces imagined-rollout error to 45% of TD-MPC2's and wins 11/12 k in {3,5,7} MSE cells. Under 12 OOD perturbations spanning dynamics shifts, action delay, and observation masking it attains the highest return in every condition (+10.2% on Finger Spin, +13.6% on Reacher Easy). Ablations show that both selective memory and the Soft-Hamiltonian prior contribute to the gains. Mechanism diagnostics support the intended design, showing bounded action-free energy drift, structured energy variation under policy rollouts, and coherent control-induced energy transfer.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages including references and technical appendix. Accepted as a poster at NeurIPS 2026. Code: https://github.com/HaoyunT/HaM_World</p></details> |
| **[WorldWeave: Rethinking Embodied Navigation through Cross-Task World Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28553v1)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Mobile embodied agents continuously alter their observations through their own motion, while external dynamics further shape future interactions. We investigate this problem through Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) and Embodied Visual Tracking (EVT), which respectively involve navigation toward a stationary goal and interaction with a moving target. We propose WorldWeave, a cross-task world model that couples shared predictive dynamics with task-specific adaptation. Instead of conditioning state transitions on heterogeneous physical actions, WorldWeave learns latent actions from observed state changes via inverse-forward dynamics, establishing a shared transition representation across tasks. The resulting future-state predictions are adaptively integrated into task-specific representations for downstream decision making. | **[X-World 1.6: Building Controllable Virtual Cities with World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00596v2)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deploying generative world models for end-to-end closed-loop evaluation and policy learning requires precise scenario control, consistent observations, and sustained generation quality during extended interactions. | **[Knowing What You Carry: Scalable Physics-Anchored World Models for Robotic Planning with Liquid Payloads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28387v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Payload-carrying robots, such as delivery robots and autonomous trucks, must choose motions that keep them and their payload safe, so their planners predict the outcome of each candidate motion. Modern planners compare thousands of candidates per step on a GPU, using world models such as batched simulators or learned predictors. Most of them assume a rigid payload, but a liquid payload keeps moving after a maneuver, and its state is rarely measured. Near a safety limit, small prediction errors can make an unsafe plan look safe. Coupled fluid simulations are accurate but too costly for every candidate, while low-order slosh models are cheap but accurate only away from resonance. We study this trade-off on a ground vehicle with a partly filled tank near its rollover limit. First, we build PLiMB (Parallel Liquid-MultiBody), a GPU-parallel slosh simulator that, to our knowledge, couples FLIP liquid two-way with many multibody vehicles for the first time. It provides training data and a high-fidelity reference. Second, we propose a scalable physics-anchored world model that combines a modal-slosh and roll prior, an estimate of the hidden liquid state from the vehicle's own motion history (no sensor in the tank), and a learned correction. Without simulating the fluid, it scores thousands of candidate plans in one batched pass within a 20 Hz control tick on one GPU. We judge each world model by the plans it leads a planner to choose. Near the rollover limit, planners that treat the liquid as a rigid payload or use published low-order slosh models repeatedly pick unsafe plans when a safe one exists. Ours never does in our standard tank and makes about half as many in a wide tank whose sloshing mode lies in the maneuver band. There, the liquid state left by earlier driving can change the best plan, and reading it from the vehicle's history halves our model's near-limit prediction error.</p></details> |  |

| **[What Must a Model Tell a Planner?
